\textbf{tangent} for the gradient questions, and a dashed reference line through the solution for
the equation-solving questions.

\section*{Exponentials 01\quad\small\texttt{(el1-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 01 (el1-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{State two key features that all graphs of the form \( y = a^x \) (where \( a > 1 \)) have in common.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: All exponential graphs pass through the point \( (0, 1) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
a^0 = 1 \text{ for any } a > 0
\end{gathered}\]
Any positive number raised to the power zero equals 1.

\textit{Step 2: The graph has a horizontal asymptote at \( y = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{As } x \to -\infty,\quad y \to 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve gets closer and closer to the \( x \)-axis but never touches it.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (1) All pass through \(\displaystyle  (0, 1) \). (2) The \(\displaystyle  x \)-axis (\(\displaystyle  y = 0 \)) is an asymptote.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 02\quad\small\texttt{(el1-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 02 (el1-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Describe the difference in shape between the graph of \( y = 4^x \) and the graph of \( y = 2^x \) for \( x > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Both graphs are increasing and pass through \( (0, 1) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4^0 = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad 2^0 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
They share the same \( y \)-intercept.

\textit{Step 2: Since \( 4 > 2 \), the graph of \( y = 4^x \) grows more steeply (faster) than \( y = 2^x \) for \( x > 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4^x > 2^x \text{ for all } x > 0
\end{gathered}\]
A larger base means a faster rate of increase.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Both pass through \(\displaystyle  (0,1) \) and are increasing, but \(\displaystyle  y = 4^x \) is steeper (grows faster) than \(\displaystyle  y = 2^x \) for \(\displaystyle  x > 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 03\quad\small\texttt{(el1-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 03 (el1-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Describe the graph of \( y = \left(\dfrac{1}{3}\right)^x \). In particular, state its \( y \)-intercept, whether it is increasing or decreasing, and the equation of its asymptote.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find the \( y \)-intercept by setting \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^0 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
\( y \)-intercept is \( (0, 1) \).

\textit{Step 2: Since the base \( \frac{1}{3} < 1 \), the function is decreasing.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{As } x \text{ increases, } \left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^x \text{ decreases towards } 0
\end{gathered}\]
Raising a fraction to a larger power makes it smaller.

\textit{Step 3: The asymptote is the \( x \)-axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{As } x \to \infty,\quad y \to 0
\end{gathered}\]
The graph approaches but never reaches \( y = 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  y \)-intercept \(\displaystyle  (0, 1) \); decreasing; asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 04\quad\small\texttt{(el1-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 04 (el1-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Explain why \( y = \left(\dfrac{1}{5}\right)^x \) can also be written as \( y = 5^{-x} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Use the rule for negative exponents.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left(\frac{1}{5}\right)^x = \frac{1}{5^x} = 5^{-x}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \frac{1}{a^x} = a^{-x} \) by the laws of indices.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \left(\frac{1}{5}\right)^x = 5^{-x} \) because raising the reciprocal to the power \(\displaystyle  x \) is the same as raising 5 to the power \(\displaystyle  -x \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 05\quad\small\texttt{(el1-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 05 (el1-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Describe the transformation that maps \( y = 3^x \) onto \( y = 3^{-x} \). What does this tell you about the relationship between the graphs of \( y = 3^x \) and \( y = \left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^x \)?}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Replacing \( x \) with \( -x \) reflects the graph in the \( y \)-axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3^{-x} \text{ is a reflection of } y = 3^x \text{ in the } y\text{-axis}
\end{gathered}\]
This is the standard horizontal reflection transformation.

\textit{Step 2: Note that \( 3^{-x} = \left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3^{-x} = \frac{1}{3^x} = \left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^x
\end{gathered}\]
So the two graphs are identical.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Reflection in the \(\displaystyle  y \)-axis. The graphs of \(\displaystyle  y = 3^x \) and \(\displaystyle  y = \left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^x \) are reflections of each other in the \(\displaystyle  y \)-axis.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 06\quad\small\texttt{(el1-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 06 (el1-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{For the function \( f(x) = 2^x \), evaluate \( f(0) \), \( f(3) \), \( f(-2) \), and \( f\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) \). Give exact answers.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Evaluate \( f(0) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(0) = 2^0 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Any base to the power zero is 1.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate \( f(3) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(3) = 2^3 = 8
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2 \times 2 \times 2 = 8 \).

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate \( f(-2) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(-2) = 2^{-2} = \frac{1}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2^{-2} = \frac{1}{2^2} = \frac{1}{4} \).

\textit{Step 4: Evaluate \( f\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = 2^{\frac{1}{2}} = \sqrt{2}
\end{gathered}\]
A power of \( \frac{1}{2} \) means square root.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f(0) = 1,\ f(3) = 8,\ f(-2) = \frac{1}{4},\ f\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = \sqrt{2} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 07\quad\small\texttt{(el1-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 07 (el1-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{On the same axes, sketch the graphs of \( y = 5^x \) and \( y = 5^{-x} \) for \( -3 \leq x \leq 3 \). Label the \( y \)-intercept and asymptote of each graph.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Both graphs pass through \( (0, 1) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
5^0 = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad 5^{-0} = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Common \( y \)-intercept at \( (0, 1) \).

\textit{Step 2: Sketch \( y = 5^x \): increasing, steeply rising for \( x > 0 \), approaching \( y = 0 \) as \( x \to -\infty \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Asymptote: } y = 0 \text{ (left side)}
\end{gathered}\]
Standard increasing exponential curve.

\textit{Step 3: Sketch \( y = 5^{-x} \): decreasing, mirror image of \( y = 5^x \) in the \( y \)-axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Asymptote: } y = 0 \text{ (right side)}
\end{gathered}\]
The two graphs are reflections of each other in the \( y \)-axis.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Both curves pass through \(\displaystyle  (0,1) \) with asymptote \(\displaystyle  y=0 \). \(\displaystyle  y=5^x \) increases; \(\displaystyle  y=5^{-x} \) decreases (its mirror image).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Exponentials 08\quad\small\texttt{(el1-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 08 (el1-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The function \( f(x) = 1.8^x \).  \\[3pt]  (a) State the coordinates of the point where the graph crosses the \( y \)-axis.  \\[3pt]  (b) Is the graph increasing or decreasing?  \\[3pt]  (c) Sketch the graph.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Set \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(0) = 1.8^0 = 1 \Rightarrow (0,\ 1)
\end{gathered}\]
All exponential graphs of the form \( a^x \) cross the \( y \)-axis at 1.

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): Since the base \( 1.8 > 1 \), the graph is increasing.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1.8 > 1 \Rightarrow \text{increasing}
\end{gathered}\]
As \( x \) increases, \( 1.8^x \) grows.

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): Sketch a smooth upward curve through \( (0,1) \), rising to the right and approaching \( y=0 \) to the left.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Asymptote: } y = 0; \quad \text{passes through } (-1,\ \tfrac{1}{1.8}) \text{ and } (1,\ 1.8)
\end{gathered}\]
Typical shape of \( y = a^x \) for \( a > 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  (0, 1) \). (b) Increasing. (c) Smooth upward curve with asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Exponentials 09\quad\small\texttt{(el1-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 09 (el1-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{State the approximate value of \( e \) and explain what is special about the function \( y = e^x \) in terms of its gradient.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: The value of \( e \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e \approx 2.718
\end{gathered}\]
\( e \) is an irrational number approximately equal to 2.718.

\textit{Step 2: The special gradient property.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{d}{dx}(e^x) = e^x
\end{gathered}\]
For the function \( y = e^x \), the gradient (derivative) at every point equals the value of the function itself. This is unique to \( e \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  e \approx 2.718 \). The gradient of \(\displaystyle  y = e^x \) equals \(\displaystyle  e^x \) at every point --- i.e., its own value.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 10\quad\small\texttt{(el1-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 10 (el1-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the gradient of \( y = e^{3x} \) at the point where \( x = 0 \). Give an exact answer.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the form \( y = e^{kx} \) with \( k = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{3x}, \quad k = 3
\end{gathered}\]
This is in the standard form \( y = e^{kx} \).

\textit{Step 2: The gradient of \( y = e^{kx} \) is \( ke^{kx} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = 3e^{3x}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply the function by \( k \) to get the gradient.

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate at \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3e^{3 \times 0} = 3e^0 = 3 \times 1 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^0 = 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Gradient \(\displaystyle  = 3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 11\quad\small\texttt{(el1-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 11 (el1-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the gradient of \( y = 4e^{2x} \) at the points \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 1 \). Give exact answers.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify \( A = 4 \) and \( k = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 4e^{2x}, \quad A = 4, \quad k = 2
\end{gathered}\]
In the form \( y = Ae^{kx} \).

\textit{Step 2: Write the gradient formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = kAe^{kx} = 2 \times 4 \times e^{2x} = 8e^{2x}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply \( A \) by \( k \) to get the coefficient of the derivative.

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate at \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
8e^{0} = 8
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^0 = 1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Evaluate at \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
8e^{2 \times 1} = 8e^2
\end{gathered}\]
Leave as an exact value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad At \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = 8 \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 1 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = 8e^2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 12\quad\small\texttt{(el1-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 12 (el1-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the gradient of \( f(x) = 7e^{5x} \) at the points \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 2 \). Leave your answers as exact values.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify \( A = 7 \) and \( k = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x) = 7e^{5x}
\end{gathered}\]
Standard form \( Ae^{kx} \).

\textit{Step 2: The gradient is \( kAe^{kx} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 5 \times 7 \times e^{5x} = 35e^{5x}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply the exponent by the coefficient.

\textit{Step 3: Gradient at \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(0) = 35e^{0} = 35
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^0 = 1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Gradient at \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(2) = 35e^{10}
\end{gathered}\]
Exact value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad At \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = 35 \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = 35e^{10} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 13\quad\small\texttt{(el1-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 13 (el1-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the gradient of \( y = 3e^{-2x} \) at the points \( x = 1 \) and \( x = -1 \). Leave your answers as exact values.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Here \( A = 3 \) and \( k = -2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3e^{-2x}
\end{gathered}\]
The exponent is negative, so the function is decreasing.

\textit{Step 2: Compute the gradient formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = (-2)(3)e^{-2x} = -6e^{-2x}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply \( k \) by \( A \).

\textit{Step 3: Gradient at \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-6e^{-2} = -\frac{6}{e^2}
\end{gathered}\]
The gradient is negative (decreasing function).

\textit{Step 4: Gradient at \( x = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-6e^{-2(-1)} = -6e^{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Substituting \( x = -1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad At \(\displaystyle  x = 1 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = -\dfrac{6}{e^2} \). At \(\displaystyle  x = -1 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = -6e^2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 14\quad\small\texttt{(el1-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 14 (el1-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the gradient of \( y = 6e^{4x} \) at the points \( x = -1 \) and \( x = 0.5 \). Leave your answers as exact values.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify \( A = 6 \), \( k = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 6e^{4x}
\end{gathered}\]
Standard form.

\textit{Step 2: Gradient formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = 4 \times 6 \times e^{4x} = 24e^{4x}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply the coefficient by the exponent.

\textit{Step 3: Gradient at \( x = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
24e^{-4} = \frac{24}{e^4}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = -1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Gradient at \( x = 0.5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
24e^{4 \times 0.5} = 24e^{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0.5 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad At \(\displaystyle  x = -1 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = \dfrac{24}{e^4} \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 0.5 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = 24e^2 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 15\quad\small\texttt{(el1-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 15 (el1-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the gradient of \( g(t) = 2.5e^{3t} \) at the points \( t = 0 \) and \( t = 4 \). Leave your answers as exact values.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify \( A = 2.5 \) and \( k = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
g(t) = 2.5e^{3t}
\end{gathered}\]
The variable is \( t \) instead of \( x \) --- the same rule applies.

\textit{Step 2: Gradient formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
g'(t) = 3 \times 2.5 \times e^{3t} = 7.5e^{3t}
\end{gathered}\]
\( kA = 3 \times 2.5 = 7.5 \).

\textit{Step 3: Gradient at \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
g'(0) = 7.5e^0 = 7.5
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^0 = 1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Gradient at \( t = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
g'(4) = 7.5e^{12}
\end{gathered}\]
Exact value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad At \(\displaystyle  t = 0 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = 7.5 \). At \(\displaystyle  t = 4 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = 7.5e^{12} \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 16\quad\small\texttt{(el1-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 16 (el1-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Three graphs are shown. Match each of the following functions to the correct graph: \( y = e^x \), \( y = 2e^x \), \( y = e^{2x} \). Justify your answers.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: All three pass through or near \( (0,1) \) --- but \( y = 2e^x \) passes through \( (0, 2) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2e^0 = 2; \quad y = e^0 = 1; \quad y = e^0 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
The graph with \( y \)-intercept 2 is \( y = 2e^x \).

\textit{Step 2: Between \( y = e^x \) and \( y = e^{2x} \): both pass through \( (0,1) \), but \( y = e^{2x} \) grows much faster.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{2x} = (e^x)^2 \text{ so it grows faster for } x > 0
\end{gathered}\]
The steeper graph is \( y = e^{2x} \); the shallower one is \( y = e^x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Graph with \(\displaystyle  y \)-intercept 2 $\to$ \(\displaystyle  y = 2e^x \). Steepest remaining graph $\to$ \(\displaystyle  y = e^{2x} \). Remaining $\to$ \(\displaystyle  y = e^x \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 17\quad\small\texttt{(el1-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 17 (el1-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Describe the transformation that maps \( y = e^x \) onto \( y = e^x + 3 \). State the \( y \)-intercept and asymptote of \( y = e^x + 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Adding 3 outside the function shifts the graph up by 3 units.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^x \to y = e^x + 3 \quad \text{(translation } +3 \text{ in } y\text{-direction)}
\end{gathered}\]
This is a vertical translation.

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0: \quad y = e^0 + 3 = 1 + 3 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( y \)-intercept is \( (0, 4) \).

\textit{Step 3: The asymptote shifts up too.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{As } x \to -\infty, \quad e^x \to 0 \Rightarrow y \to 3
\end{gathered}\]
The original asymptote \( y = 0 \) shifts to \( y = 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Vertical translation \(\displaystyle  +3 \). \(\displaystyle  y \)-intercept \(\displaystyle  (0, 4) \); asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 3 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 18\quad\small\texttt{(el1-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 18 (el1-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Describe the transformation that maps \( y = e^x \) onto \( y = e^{x-2} \). State the \( y \)-intercept and the asymptote of \( y = e^{x-2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Replacing \( x \) with \( x - 2 \) shifts the graph 2 units to the right.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^x \to y = e^{x-2} \quad \text{(translation } +2 \text{ in } x\text{-direction)}
\end{gathered}\]
This is a horizontal translation of \( +2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0: \quad y = e^{0-2} = e^{-2} = \frac{1}{e^2}
\end{gathered}\]
The \( y \)-intercept is at \( \left(0, \frac{1}{e^2}\right) \).

\textit{Step 3: The asymptote remains \( y = 0 \) (horizontal translation does not move the asymptote).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{As } x \to -\infty, \quad y \to 0
\end{gathered}\]
The asymptote is unchanged.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Horizontal translation \(\displaystyle  +2 \). \(\displaystyle  y \)-intercept \(\displaystyle  \left(0, \dfrac{1}{e^2}\right) \); asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 19\quad\small\texttt{(el1-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 19 (el1-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{For the function \( y = 3e^x \):  \\[3pt]  (a) State the \( y \)-intercept.  \\[3pt]  (b) Describe the transformation from \( y = e^x \).  \\[3pt]  (c) Find the gradient of \( y = 3e^x \) at \( x = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Set \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3e^0 = 3 \Rightarrow (0,\ 3)
\end{gathered}\]
\( y \)-intercept is \( (0, 3) \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): Multiplying by 3 stretches vertically by scale factor 3.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^x \to y = 3e^x \quad \text{(vertical stretch, scale factor 3)}
\end{gathered}\]
Every \( y \)-value is multiplied by 3.

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): Gradient of \( y = 3e^x \) is \( 3e^x \) (since \( k = 1 \)).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx}\Big|_{x=0} = 3e^0 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
The gradient equals the function value everywhere for \( y = 3e^x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  (0, 3) \). (b) Vertical stretch by scale factor 3. (c) Gradient \(\displaystyle  = 3 \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 20\quad\small\texttt{(el1-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 20 (el1-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{On the same axes, sketch \( y = e^x \), \( y = e^x - 1 \), and \( y = -e^x \). Label all \( y \)-intercepts and asymptotes.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: \( y = e^x \): passes through \( (0,1) \), increasing, asymptote \( y = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-intercept: } (0,1); \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Standard exponential.

\textit{Step 2: \( y = e^x - 1 \): same shape, shifted down 1 unit. Passes through \( (0, 0) \); asymptote \( y = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-intercept: } (0,0); \quad \text{asymptote: } y = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Vertical translation of \( -1 \). Note it crosses the origin!

\textit{Step 3: \( y = -e^x \): reflection of \( y = e^x \) in the \( x \)-axis. Passes through \( (0,-1) \); decreasing; asymptote \( y = 0 \) (approached from below).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-intercept: } (0,-1); \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The curve lies entirely below the \( x \)-axis.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  y=e^x \): \(\displaystyle  (0,1) \), asymptote \(\displaystyle  y=0 \). \(\displaystyle  y=e^x-1 \): \(\displaystyle  (0,0) \), asymptote \(\displaystyle  y=-1 \). \(\displaystyle  y=-e^x \): \(\displaystyle  (0,-1) \), asymptote \(\displaystyle  y=0 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 21\quad\small\texttt{(el1-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 21 (el1-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The graph of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) has gradient \( 6e^{3x} \). Find the values of \( A \) and \( k \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: The gradient of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) is \( kAe^{kx} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = kAe^{kx}
\end{gathered}\]
Standard differentiation rule for this form.

\textit{Step 2: Match with the given gradient \( 6e^{3x} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
kA = 6 \quad \text{and} \quad k = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Equate the exponents and the coefficients.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( A \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = \frac{6}{k} = \frac{6}{3} = 2
\end{gathered}\]
\( kA = 6 \) and \( k = 3 \Rightarrow A = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  k = 3,\ A = 2 \), so \(\displaystyle  y = 2e^{3x} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 22\quad\small\texttt{(el1-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 22 (el1-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The graph of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) has gradient \( 20e^{4x} \). Find \( A \) and \( k \), then find the exact \( y \)-coordinates when \( x = 0 \) and \( x = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Gradient of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) is \( kAe^{kx} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
kAe^{kx} = 20e^{4x}
\end{gathered}\]
Match the two expressions.

\textit{Step 2: Match the exponent: \( k = 4 \). Match the coefficient: \( kA = 20 \Rightarrow A = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = 4, \quad A = 5
\end{gathered}\]
So \( y = 5e^{4x} \).

\textit{Step 3: \( y \)-coordinate at \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 5e^0 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
The \( y \)-intercept.

\textit{Step 4: \( y \)-coordinate at \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 5e^{12}
\end{gathered}\]
Exact value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  A = 5,\ k = 4 \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \): \(\displaystyle  y = 5 \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 3 \): \(\displaystyle  y = 5e^{12} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 23\quad\small\texttt{(el1-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 23 (el1-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The graph of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) passes through \( (0, 4) \) and has gradient \( 12 \) at \( x = 0 \). Find \( A \) and \( k \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use the point \( (0, 4) \) to find \( A \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
Ae^{k \times 0} = Ae^0 = A = 4
\end{gathered}\]
At \( x = 0 \), \( y = A \).

\textit{Step 2: Use the gradient at \( x = 0 \) to find \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
kAe^{0} = kA = k \times 4 = 12 \Rightarrow k = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Gradient at \( x = 0 \) is \( kA \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  A = 4,\ k = 3 \), so \(\displaystyle  y = 4e^{3x} \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 24\quad\small\texttt{(el1-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 24 (el1-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The graph of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) passes through \( (0, 6) \) and the gradient at \( x = 0 \) is \( -18 \). Find \( A \) and \( k \), and state whether the function is increasing or decreasing.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: From the \( y \)-intercept: \( A = 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y(0) = Ae^0 = A = 6
\end{gathered}\]
At \( x = 0 \), \( e^0 = 1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Gradient at \( x = 0 \) is \( kA = -18 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k \times 6 = -18 \Rightarrow k = -3
\end{gathered}\]
A negative \( k \) means the function is decreasing.

\textit{Step 3: State the nature.}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = -3 < 0 \Rightarrow \text{decreasing}
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( k < 0 \), the function decays exponentially.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  A = 6,\ k = -3 \), so \(\displaystyle  y = 6e^{-3x} \). The function is decreasing.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 25\quad\small\texttt{(el1-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 25 (el1-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The graph of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) has gradient \( 15e^{5x} \) at point \( x = 2 \). Find \( A \) and \( k \), and hence find the exact \( y \)-coordinates at \( x = 4 \) and \( x = 10 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: The gradient function is \( kAe^{kx} = 15e^{5x} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = 5, \quad kA = 15 \Rightarrow A = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Match coefficients and exponents.

\textit{Step 2: So \( y = 3e^{5x} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3e^{5x}
\end{gathered}\]
The equation of the exponential function.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \) at \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3e^{20}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 5 \times 4 = 20 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find \( y \) at \( x = 10 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3e^{50}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 5 \times 10 = 50 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  A = 3,\ k = 5 \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \): \(\displaystyle  y = 3e^{20} \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 10 \): \(\displaystyle  y = 3e^{50} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 26\quad\small\texttt{(el1-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 26 (el1-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Sketch the graph of \( y = 2e^{-x} \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept and asymptote. Describe the two transformations from \( y = e^x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Transformation 1: replace \( x \) with \( -x \) to get \( y = e^{-x} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^x \to y = e^{-x} \quad \text{(reflection in } y\text{-axis)}
\end{gathered}\]
Produces a decreasing exponential.

\textit{Step 2: Transformation 2: multiply by 2 to get \( y = 2e^{-x} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{-x} \to y = 2e^{-x} \quad \text{(vertical stretch, scale factor 2)}
\end{gathered}\]
Doubles all \( y \)-values.

\textit{Step 3: Label the \( y \)-intercept and asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-intercept: } (0,\ 2); \quad \text{asymptote: } y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2e^0 = 2 \). As \( x \to \infty, y \to 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  y \)-intercept \(\displaystyle  (0, 2) \); asymptote \(\displaystyle  y = 0 \). Transformations: reflection in \(\displaystyle  y \)-axis, then vertical stretch factor 2.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Exponentials 27\quad\small\texttt{(el1-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 27 (el1-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{For the graph of \( y = 5e^{2x} - 3 \):  \\[3pt]  (a) State the asymptote.  \\[3pt]  (b) Find the \( y \)-intercept.  \\[3pt]  (c) State the range of the function.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): As \( x \to -\infty \), \( 5e^{2x} \to 0 \), so \( y \to -3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Asymptote: } y = -3
\end{gathered}\]
The \( -3 \) shifts the asymptote from \( y=0 \) to \( y=-3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): Set \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 5e^0 - 3 = 5 - 3 = 2 \Rightarrow (0,\ 2)
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^0 = 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): Since \( 5e^{2x} > 0 \) always, we have \( y > -3 \) always.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y > -3 \quad \text{(i.e., range is } (-3, \infty))
\end{gathered}\]
The function never reaches its asymptote.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  y = -3 \). (b) \(\displaystyle  (0, 2) \). (c) \(\displaystyle  y > -3 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 28\quad\small\texttt{(el1-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 28 (el1-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The function \( f(x) = Ae^{kx} \) models the temperature (\textdegree C) of a liquid cooling over time \( x \) (minutes). At \( x = 0 \) the temperature is 80\textdegree C, and the rate of cooling at \( x = 0 \) is \( -8 \) \textdegree C/min. Find \( A \) and \( k \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use \( f(0) = 80 \) to find \( A \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(0) = Ae^0 = A = 80
\end{gathered}\]
The initial temperature gives \( A \).

\textit{Step 2: The rate of cooling is the gradient. At \( x = 0 \), gradient = \( kA = -8 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k \times 80 = -8 \Rightarrow k = -\frac{1}{10} = -0.1
\end{gathered}\]
A negative gradient means the temperature is falling.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  A = 80,\ k = -0.1 \), so \(\displaystyle  f(x) = 80e^{-0.1x} \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Exponentials 29\quad\small\texttt{(el1-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 29 (el1-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A population is modelled by \( P = 500e^{0.04t} \), where \( t \) is time in years.  \\[3pt]  (a) State the initial population.  \\[3pt]  (b) Find the rate of growth of the population at \( t = 0 \).  \\[3pt]  (c) Is the population increasing or decreasing? Give a reason.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Set \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P(0) = 500e^0 = 500
\end{gathered}\]
Initial population is 500.

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): Rate of growth = gradient = \( kA e^{kt} \). At \( t = 0 \):}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.04 \times 500 \times e^0 = 20 \text{ individuals/year}
\end{gathered}\]
The population grows at 20 per year initially.

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): Since \( k = 0.04 > 0 \), the population is increasing.}
\[\begin{gathered}
k > 0 \Rightarrow P \text{ increases as } t \text{ increases}
\end{gathered}\]
A positive exponent means exponential growth.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 500. (b) 20 individuals/year. (c) Increasing, because \(\displaystyle  k = 0.04 > 0 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 30\quad\small\texttt{(el1-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 30 (el1-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the gradient of \( y = 8e^{-3x} \) at the points \( x = -2 \) and \( x = 0.5 \). Leave your answers as exact values.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify \( A = 8 \) and \( k = -3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 8e^{-3x}
\end{gathered}\]
Decreasing exponential.

\textit{Step 2: Gradient formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = (-3)(8)e^{-3x} = -24e^{-3x}
\end{gathered}\]
\( kA = -24 \).

\textit{Step 3: Gradient at \( x = -2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-24e^{-3(-2)} = -24e^{6}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = -2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Gradient at \( x = 0.5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-24e^{-3(0.5)} = -24e^{-1.5} = -\frac{24}{e^{1.5}}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 0.5 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad At \(\displaystyle  x = -2 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = -24e^6 \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 0.5 \): gradient \(\displaystyle  = -\dfrac{24}{e^{3/2}} \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 31\quad\small\texttt{(el1-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 31 (el1-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The graph of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) passes through the points \( (0, 3) \) and has gradient \( 9 \) at \( x = 0 \).  \\[3pt]  (a) Find the values of \( A \) and \( k \).  \\[3pt]  (b) Write down the gradient function.  \\[3pt]  (c) Find the exact gradient at \( x = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): From the \( y \)-intercept \( (0, 3) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = 3
\end{gathered}\]
\( y(0) = A = 3 \).

\textit{Step 2: From gradient \( = 9 \) at \( x = 0 \): \( kA = 9 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k \times 3 = 9 \Rightarrow k = 3
\end{gathered}\]
So \( y = 3e^{3x} \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (b): Gradient function.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = 9e^{3x}
\end{gathered}\]
\( kA = 9 \), multiplied by \( e^{3x} \).

\textit{Step 4: Part (c): Gradient at \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
9e^{6}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 3 \times 2 = 6 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  A = 3,\ k = 3 \). (b) \(\displaystyle  \frac{dy}{dx} = 9e^{3x} \). (c) Gradient \(\displaystyle  = 9e^6 \).
\end{answerbox}

\small The exponential function plotted with key points marked and the tangent (dashed) showing the gradient.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 32\quad\small\texttt{(el1-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 32 (el1-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{(a) On the same axes, sketch the graphs of \( y = e^x \) and \( y = 4 - e^x \) for \( -2 \leq x \leq 2 \). Label the intercepts of each graph.  \\[3pt]   \\[3pt]  (b) Find the coordinates of the point where the two graphs intersect.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Key features of \( y = e^x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-intercept: }(0,1); \quad \text{asymptote: }y=0
\end{gathered}\]
Standard increasing exponential.

\textit{Step 2: Key features of \( y = 4 - e^x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\text{-intercept: }(0,\ 4-1)=(0,3); \quad x\text{-intercept: }e^x=4 \Rightarrow x = \ln 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( y = 0 \Rightarrow e^x = 4 \Rightarrow x = \ln 4 \approx 1.39 \). Asymptote as \( x \to -\infty \): \( y \to 4 \).

\textit{Step 3: Part (b): Set the equations equal.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 4 - e^x \Rightarrow 2e^x = 4 \Rightarrow e^x = 2 \Rightarrow x = \ln 2
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \) by collecting \( e^x \) terms.

\textit{Step 4: Find \( y \) at the intersection.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = e^{\ln 2} = 2 \Rightarrow \text{intersection at } (\ln 2,\ 2)
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{\ln 2} = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (b) Intersection at \(\displaystyle  (\ln 2,\ 2) \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 33\quad\small\texttt{(el1-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 33 (el1-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The mass \( M \) grams of a radioactive substance at time \( t \) hours is modelled by \( M = 120e^{-0.05t} \).  \\[3pt]  (a) Find the initial mass.  \\[3pt]  (b) Find the rate of decay at \( t = 0 \).  \\[3pt]  (c) Find the exact mass when \( t = 20 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Set \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
M(0) = 120e^0 = 120 \text{ g}
\end{gathered}\]
The initial mass is 120 g.

\textit{Step 2: Part (b): Rate of decay = gradient. \( \frac{dM}{dt} = kA e^{kt} \). At \( t = 0 \):}
\[\begin{gathered}
kA = (-0.05)(120) = -6 \text{ g/hour}
\end{gathered}\]
Negative because the mass is decreasing.

\textit{Step 3: Part (c): Substitute \( t = 20 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
M(20) = 120e^{-0.05 \times 20} = 120e^{-1} = \frac{120}{e}
\end{gathered}\]
\( -0.05 \times 20 = -1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 120 g. (b) \(\displaystyle  -6 \) g/hour. (c) \(\displaystyle  M = \dfrac{120}{e} \) g.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 34\quad\small\texttt{(el1-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 34 (el1-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The graph of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) has gradient \( 12e^{2x} \) at the point \( x = 2 \). Find \( A \) and \( k \), and hence find the exact \( y \)-coordinates when \( x = 4 \) and \( x = 12 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: The gradient of \( y = Ae^{kx} \) is \( kAe^{kx} \). Match with \( 12e^{2x} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k = 2, \quad kA = 12 \Rightarrow A = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Equate exponents and coefficients.

\textit{Step 2: So \( y = 6e^{2x} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 6e^{2x}
\end{gathered}\]
The equation of the curve.

\textit{Step 3: \( y \)-coordinate at \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 6e^{8}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2 \times 4 = 8 \).

\textit{Step 4: \( y \)-coordinate at \( x = 12 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 6e^{24}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2 \times 12 = 24 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  A = 6,\ k = 2 \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \): \(\displaystyle  y = 6e^8 \). At \(\displaystyle  x = 12 \): \(\displaystyle  y = 6e^{24} \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 35\quad\small\texttt{(el1-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 35 (el1-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{(a) Sketch the graph of \( y = 3e^{2x} - 6 \), labelling the \( y \)-intercept, any \( x \)-intercept, and the asymptote.  \\[3pt]   \\[3pt]  (b) State the range of the function.  \\[3pt]   \\[3pt]  (c) Find the gradient of the curve at \( x = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Part (a): Find the asymptote.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{As } x \to -\infty, \quad 3e^{2x} \to 0 \Rightarrow y \to -6
\end{gathered}\]
Asymptote: \( y = -6 \).

\textit{Step 2: Find the \( y \)-intercept.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0: \quad y = 3e^0 - 6 = 3 - 6 = -3 \Rightarrow (0,\ -3)
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^0 = 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( x \)-intercept (where \( y = 0 \)).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3e^{2x} = 6 \Rightarrow e^{2x} = 2 \Rightarrow 2x = \ln 2 \Rightarrow x = \frac{\ln 2}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Takes logarithms to solve for \( x \).

\textit{Step 4: Sketch: increasing curve from asymptote \( y = -6 \), crossing \( x \)-axis at \( x = \frac{\ln 2}{2} \approx 0.35 \), \( y \)-intercept at \( -3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{x-intercept: } \left(\tfrac{\ln 2}{2},\ 0\right)
\end{gathered}\]
Label all three key points/lines.

\textit{Step 5: Part (b): Since \( 3e^{2x} > 0 \) always, \( y > -6 \) always.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Range: } y > -6
\end{gathered}\]
The function never reaches its asymptote.

\textit{Step 6: Part (c): Gradient function.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{dy}{dx} = 2 \times 3 \times e^{2x} = 6e^{2x}
\end{gathered}\]
\( kA = 2 \times 3 = 6 \).

\textit{Step 7: Gradient at \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
6e^{0} = 6
\end{gathered}\]
The curve has gradient 6 at the \( y \)-intercept.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) Asymptote \(\displaystyle  y=-6 \), \(\displaystyle  y \)-intercept \(\displaystyle  (0,-3) \), \(\displaystyle  x \)-intercept \(\displaystyle  \left(\frac{\ln 2}{2}, 0\right) \). (b) \(\displaystyle  y > -6 \). (c) Gradient \(\displaystyle  = 6 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Exponentials 38\quad\small\texttt{(el1-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 38 (el1-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Simplify \( \frac{5^7}{5^3} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Subtract indices}
\[\begin{gathered}
5^{7-3} = 5^4 = 625
\end{gathered}\]
Dividing powers of the same base subtracts the exponents: \( \dfrac{a^m}{a^n} = a^{m-n} \). Then \( 5^4 = 5 \times 5 \times 5 \times 5 = 625 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 5^4 = 625\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 39\quad\small\texttt{(el1-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 39 (el1-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( 4^{-2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Negative index}
\[\begin{gathered}
4^{-2} = \frac{1}{4^2} = \frac{1}{16}
\end{gathered}\]
A negative index means take the reciprocal: \( a^{-n} = \dfrac{1}{a^{n}} \). Pitfall: \( 4^{-2} \) is not \( -8 \) or \( -16 \) --- the minus sign in the exponent never makes the result negative for a positive base.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \frac{1}{16}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 40\quad\small\texttt{(el1-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 40 (el1-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( 8^{\frac{2}{3}} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Fractional index}
\[\begin{gathered}
8^{2/3} = (8^{1/3})^2 = 2^2 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Cube root of 8 is 2, then square.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 4
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 42\quad\small\texttt{(el1-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 42 (el1-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = 3^{-x} \). Describe the transformation from \( y = 3^x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Reflection in y-axis}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3^{-x} = \left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^x
\end{gathered}\]
Exponential decay.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Reflection in y-axis; exponential decay
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 43\quad\small\texttt{(el1-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 43 (el1-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( 2^x = 16 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write 16 as power of 2}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^x = 2^4 \implies x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite the right-hand side using the same base as the left, then equate the exponents (because \( a^x \) is a one-to-one function for \( a > 0,\ a \neq 1 \)). Since \( 16 = 2^4 \), the equation forces \( x = 4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 4\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 44\quad\small\texttt{(el1-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 44 (el1-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( 3^x = \frac{1}{27} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write as power of 3}
\[\begin{gathered}
3^x = 3^{-3} \implies x = -3
\end{gathered}\]
27 = 3\textasciicircum 3, so 1/27 = 3\textasciicircum \{-3\}.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = -3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 45\quad\small\texttt{(el1-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 45 (el1-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 5^{2x} = 125 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write 125 as power of 5}
\[\begin{gathered}
5^{2x} = 5^3 \implies 2x = 3 \implies x = \frac{3}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite both sides with the same base, then equate the exponents. Note \( 125 = 5^3 \), and dividing both sides of \( 2x = 3 \) by 2 gives the answer as a fraction --- leave it as \( \tfrac{3}{2} \) for an exact answer.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = \frac{3}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 46\quad\small\texttt{(el1-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 46 (el1-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{The value of an investment is modelled by \( V = 1000 \times 1.05^t \) where \( t \) is years. Find the value after 10 years.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Substitute t = 10}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 1000 \times 1.05^{10} = 1000 \times 1.6289 = 1628.89
\end{gathered}\]
For compound growth at \( 5\% \) per year, each year multiplies the value by \( 1.05 \), so after 10 years the multiplier is \( 1.05^{10} \approx 1.6289 \). Keep at least 4 d.p. on the intermediate multiplier before rounding the final answer to 2 d.p.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 1628.89 pounds
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 47\quad\small\texttt{(el1-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 47 (el1-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Write \( e^3 \) to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Calculator}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^3 = 20.1
\end{gathered}\]
Using the \( e^x \) button on a calculator: \( e^3 \approx 20.0855 \), which rounds to \( 20.1 \) (3 s.f.). Recall \( e \approx 2.718 \) is the natural base --- the unique value for which the gradient of \( y = e^x \) equals \( e^x \) itself.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 20.1
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 48\quad\small\texttt{(el1-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 48 (el1-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Sketch \( y = e^x \) and \( y = e^{-x} \) on the same axes. State their point of intersection.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Intersection}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = e^{-x} \implies e^{2x} = 1 \implies x = 0, y = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Multiplying both sides by \( e^x \) (which is always positive) gives \( e^{2x} = 1 \), so \( 2x = 0 \). Both curves pass through \( (0, 1) \) because any base to the power 0 is 1 --- and they are reflections of each other in the \( y \)-axis, so this is their only meeting point.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Intersection at (0, 1)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 49\quad\small\texttt{(el1-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 49 (el1-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Simplify \( e^{2x} \times e^{3x} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Add indices}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{2x+3x} = e^{5x}
\end{gathered}\]
When multiplying powers of the same base, add the exponents: \( a^m \times a^n = a^{m+n} \). The base \( e \) behaves like any other base under the laws of indices.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle e^{5x}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 50\quad\small\texttt{(el1-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 50 (el1-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Simplify \( \frac{e^{4x}}{e^x} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Subtract indices}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{4x-x} = e^{3x}
\end{gathered}\]
Dividing powers of the same base subtracts the exponents: \( \dfrac{a^m}{a^n} = a^{m-n} \). Here \( 4x - x = 3x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle e^{3x}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 51\quad\small\texttt{(el1-051)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 51 (el1-051)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A population of bacteria doubles every 3 hours. Starting with 500 bacteria, find the population after 12 hours.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Number of doublings}
\[\begin{gathered}
12 \div 3 = 4 \text{ doublings}
\end{gathered}\]
Each doubling period is 3 hours, so in 12 hours the population doubles \( \tfrac{12}{3} = 4 \) times. This is the exponent in the growth factor.

\textit{Step 2: Calculate}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 500 \times 2^4 = 500 \times 16 = 8000
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply the starting amount by \( 2 \) for each doubling, i.e. by \( 2^4 = 16 \). The underlying model is \( P = 500 \times 2^{t/3} \) --- the discrete-doubling viewpoint is just this evaluated at \( t = 12 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 8000 bacteria
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 52\quad\small\texttt{(el1-052)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 52 (el1-052)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 4^x = 8 \). Give your answer as a fraction.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write both as powers of 2}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2^2)^x = 2^3 \implies 2^{2x} = 2^3 \implies 2x = 3 \implies x = \frac{3}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite both sides with the same base so the exponents can be equated. Using the power-of-a-power rule \( (a^m)^n = a^{mn} \), \( 4^x = (2^2)^x = 2^{2x} \). Pitfall: do not write \( 4^x \) as \( 2^x \cdot 2 \) --- the entire base is squared, not multiplied.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = \frac{3}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 53\quad\small\texttt{(el1-053)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 53 (el1-053)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 9^x = 27 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write as powers of 3}
\[\begin{gathered}
(3^2)^x = 3^3 \implies 3^{2x} = 3^3 \implies x = \frac{3}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Express both sides with base 3 using \( 9 = 3^2 \) and \( 27 = 3^3 \). Apply \( (a^m)^n = a^{mn} \), then equate exponents: \( 2x = 3 \Rightarrow x = \tfrac{3}{2} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = \frac{3}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 54\quad\small\texttt{(el1-054)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 54 (el1-054)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Describe the transformation that maps \( y = e^x \) to \( y = e^x + 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Translation}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Translation 3 units up}
\end{gathered}\]
Asymptote moves from y = 0 to y = 3.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Translation 3 units up; new asymptote y = 3
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 55\quad\small\texttt{(el1-055)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 55 (el1-055)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Describe the transformation that maps \( y = e^x \) to \( y = e^{x-2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Translation}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Translation 2 units right}
\end{gathered}\]
Replacing \( x \) with \( x - 2 \) inside the function shifts the graph 2 units in the positive \( x \)-direction. The asymptote stays at \( y = 0 \) since horizontal translations do not move horizontal asymptotes. Pitfall: \( x - 2 \) gives a shift to the right, not the left.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Translation 2 units right
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 56\quad\small\texttt{(el1-056)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 56 (el1-056)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Describe the transformation that maps \( y = e^x \) to \( y = 2e^x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Stretch}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Vertical stretch, scale factor 2}
\end{gathered}\]
y-intercept moves from 1 to 2.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Vertical stretch scale factor 2
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 57\quad\small\texttt{(el1-057)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 57 (el1-057)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{A radioactive substance decays so that the mass \( m \) grams after \( t \) years is \( m = 200e^{-0.1t} \). Find the mass after 5 years.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Substitute}
\[\begin{gathered}
m = 200e^{-0.5} = 200 \times 0.6065 = 121.3 \text{ g}
\end{gathered}\]
Put \( t = 5 \) into the model: the exponent becomes \( -0.1 \times 5 = -0.5 \), and \( e^{-0.5} \approx 0.6065 \). The base \( e \) appears because radioactive decay is a continuous process --- the rate of decay is proportional to the current mass.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 121.3 g
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 58\quad\small\texttt{(el1-058)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 58 (el1-058)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( e^x = 5 \). Give your answer in terms of \( \ln \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Take ln of both sides}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 5
\end{gathered}\]
The natural log \( \ln \) is the inverse of \( e^x \), so \( \ln(e^x) = x \). Taking \( \ln \) of both sides of \( e^x = 5 \) gives \( x = \ln 5 \) --- leave this exact rather than using \( \approx 1.609 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = ln 5\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 59\quad\small\texttt{(el1-059)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 59 (el1-059)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( e^{2x} = 7 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x = \ln 7 \implies x = \frac{\ln 7}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Applying \( \ln \) to both sides uses \( \ln(e^{2x}) = 2x \). Then divide by 2 to isolate \( x \). Pitfall: do not split \( \ln 7 \) into \( \ln 3 + \ln 4 \) etc --- it is already in its simplest exact form.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = \frac{\ln 7}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 60\quad\small\texttt{(el1-060)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 60 (el1-060)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 3e^x - 6 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Rearrange}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 2 \implies x = \ln 2
\end{gathered}\]
Isolate \( e^x \) first: add 6 to both sides and divide by 3 to get \( e^x = 2 \). Then take \( \ln \) of both sides, using \( \ln(e^x) = x \), to obtain \( x = \ln 2 \) (an exact answer).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = ln 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 61\quad\small\texttt{(el1-061)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 61 (el1-061)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( 27^{\frac{1}{3}} + 16^{\frac{1}{4}} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Evaluate each}
\[\begin{gathered}
3 + 2 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Cube root of 27 is 3; fourth root of 16 is 2.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 5

\section*{Exponentials 62\quad\small\texttt{(el1-062)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 62 (el1-062)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Simplify \( (2^3)^4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Multiply indices}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^{12} = 4096
\end{gathered}\]
When raising a power to a power, multiply the indices: \( (a^m)^n = a^{mn} \). So \( (2^3)^4 = 2^{3 \times 4} = 2^{12} \). Pitfall: do not add the indices --- that rule is for multiplying powers with the same base, not nesting them.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 2^{12} = 4096\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 63\quad\small\texttt{(el1-063)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 63 (el1-063)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{The graph of \( y = a \times b^x \) passes through \( (0, 3) \) and \( (2, 48) \). Find \( a \) and \( b \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: At x = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
a \times b^0 = 3 \implies a = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Any base to the power 0 is 1, so the \( y \)-intercept of \( y = ab^x \) is just \( a \). The point \( (0, 3) \) therefore pins down \( a \) immediately.

\textit{Step 2: At x = 2}
\[\begin{gathered}
3b^2 = 48 \implies b^2 = 16 \implies b = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Positive since exponential.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle a = 3, b = 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Exponentials 64\quad\small\texttt{(el1-064)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 64 (el1-064)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( 2^{x+1} = 32 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write 32 as power of 2}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^{x+1} = 2^5 \implies x + 1 = 5 \implies x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Express \( 32 = 2^5 \) so both sides share base 2, then equate the exponents. Pitfall: the exponent is the entire expression \( x + 1 \), so subtract 1 (not 5) when solving for \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 65\quad\small\texttt{(el1-065)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 65 (el1-065)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The temperature \( T \) of a cooling object is modelled by \( T = 20 + 80e^{-0.05t} \). Find: (a) the initial temperature; (b) the temperature after 20 minutes; (c) the long-term temperature.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) t = 0}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 20 + 80 = 100^\circ
\end{gathered}\]
At \( t = 0 \), \( e^{-0.05 \times 0} = e^0 = 1 \), so the model collapses to \( T = 20 + 80 \). This is the starting temperature of the object.

\textit{Step 2: (b) t = 20}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 20 + 80e^{-1} = 20 + 29.4 = 49.4^\circ
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( t = 20 \): the exponent becomes \( -0.05 \times 20 = -1 \), and \( e^{-1} = \tfrac{1}{e} \approx 0.3679 \), giving \( 80 \times 0.3679 \approx 29.4 \). The natural base \( e \) arises because cooling is a continuous process (Newton's Law of Cooling).

\textit{Step 3: (c) As t -> infinity}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{-0.05t} \to 0 \implies T \to 20^\circ
\end{gathered}\]
Room temperature.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 100 degrees (b) 49.4 degrees (c) 20 degrees
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 66\quad\small\texttt{(el1-066)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 66 (el1-066)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Simplify \( \frac{e^{3x} \times e^{2x}}{e^{4x}} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Index laws}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{e^{5x}}{e^{4x}} = e^{x}
\end{gathered}\]
First combine the numerator using \( a^m \times a^n = a^{m+n} \): \( e^{3x} \times e^{2x} = e^{5x} \). Then divide using \( \dfrac{a^m}{a^n} = a^{m-n} \): \( e^{5x - 4x} = e^{x} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle e^x\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 67\quad\small\texttt{(el1-067)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 67 (el1-067)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( e^{2x} - 5e^x + 6 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let u = e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
u^2 - 5u + 6 = 0 \implies (u-2)(u-3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Notice \( e^{2x} = (e^x)^2 \), so the substitution \( u = e^x \) turns the equation into a standard quadratic in \( u \). Factorise to find the values of \( u \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 2 \implies x = \ln 2; \quad e^x = 3 \implies x = \ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Reverse the substitution: each root of the quadratic gives an equation in \( x \) which is solved by taking \( \ln \) of both sides. Both roots are valid because \( u = e^x > 0 \) for all real \( x \), so neither root is rejected.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = ln 2 or x = ln 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 68\quad\small\texttt{(el1-068)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 68 (el1-068)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( e^{2x} - 7e^x + 10 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let u = e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
(u-2)(u-5) = 0 \implies u = 2 \text{ or } u = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Substituting \( u = e^x \) (so \( e^{2x} = u^2 \)) gives the quadratic \( u^2 - 7u + 10 = 0 \), which factorises to \( (u-2)(u-5) = 0 \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 2 \text{ or } x = \ln 5
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of each value of \( u \) using \( \ln(e^x) = x \). Both values of \( u \) are positive so both yield valid real \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = ln 2 or x = ln 5\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 69\quad\small\texttt{(el1-069)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 69 (el1-069)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Write \( y = 3 \times 2^x \) in the form \( y = ae^{kx} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite 2\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^x = e^{x \ln 2}
\end{gathered}\]
Since 2 = e\textasciicircum \{ln 2\}.

\textit{Step 2: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3e^{(\ln 2)x}
\end{gathered}\]
So a = 3, k = ln 2.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle y = 3e^{(ln 2)x}; a = 3, k = ln 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Exponentials 70\quad\small\texttt{(el1-070)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Exponentials 70 (el1-070)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A car depreciates in value according to \( V = 15000 \times 0.85^t \) where \( t \) is years. (a) Find the value after 3 years. (b) Find when the value drops below 5000.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute t = 3}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 15000 \times 0.85^3 = 15000 \times 0.6141 = 9211
\end{gathered}\]
With a depreciation rate of \( 15\% \) per year, the value is multiplied by \( 0.85 \) annually. After 3 years the multiplier is \( 0.85^3 \approx 0.6141 \).

\textit{Step 2: (b) Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.85^t = \frac{5000}{15000} = \frac{1}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides of \( 15000 \times 0.85^t = 5000 \) by 15000 to isolate the exponential. This is the standard first step before taking logs.

\textit{Step 3: Take logs}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln(1/3)}{\ln 0.85} = \frac{-1.099}{-0.1625} = 6.76
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides; the rule \( \ln(a^t) = t \ln a \) brings \( t \) down, giving \( t \ln 0.85 = \ln \tfrac{1}{3} \). Both logs are negative (since their arguments are between 0 and 1), and the negatives cancel when dividing --- so \( t \) is positive, as expected.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) 9211 pounds (b) After approximately 6.76 years
\end{answerbox}
